Low Degree Hardness for Broadcasting on Trees
Han Huang Elchanan Mossel

Abstract
. We study the low-degree hardness of broadcasting on trees. Broadcasting on trees has been extensively studied in statistical physics, in computational biology in relation to phylogenetic reconstruction and in statistics and computer science in the context of block model inference, and as a simple data model for algorithms that may require depth for inference. The inference of the root can be carried by celebrated Belief Propagation (BP) algorithm which achieves Bayes-optimal performance. Despite the fact that this algorithm runs in linear time (using real operations), recent works indicated that this algorithm in fact requires high level of complexity. Moitra, Mossel and Sandon constructed a chain for which estimating the root better than random (for a typical input) is N ​ C ​ 1 NC1 complete. Kohler and Mossel constructed chains such that for trees with N N leaves, recovering the root better than random requires a polynomial of degree N Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) N^{\Omega(1)} . Both works above asked if such complexity bounds hold in general below the celebrated Kesten-Stigum bound. In this work, we prove that this is indeed the case for low degree polynomials. We show that for the broadcast problem using any Markov chain on trees with n n leaves, below the Kesten Stigum bound, any O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) O(\log n) degree polynomial has vanishing correlation with the root. Our result is one of the first low-degree lower bound that is proved in a setting that is not based or easily reduced to a product measure.



1.
Introduction

Understanding the computational complexity inference problems of random instances has been extensively studies in different research areas such including statistics, cryptography, computational complexity, computational learning theory and statistical physics.
The emerging field of research is mainly devoted to the study of computational-to-statistical gaps.

Recently, low-degree polynomials have emerged as a popular tool for predicting computational-to-statistical gaps. Our work follows
[
14
]
in studying the polynomial hardness of broadcasting on trees.

As explained in
[
14
]
:
“
Computational-to-statistical gaps are situations where it is impossible for polynomial time algorithms to estimate a desired quantity of interest from the data, even though computationally inefficient (“information-theoretic”) algorithms can succeed at the same task.
Heuristics based on low-degree polynomials have been used in the context of Bayesian estimation and testing problems and partially motivated by connections with (lower bounds for) the powerful Sum-of-Squares proof system.
More specifically, a recent line of work (e.g.
[
12
,
11
,
15
,
2
,
8
,
16
,
10
,
4
,
25
]
) showed that a suitable “low-degree heuristic” can be used to predict computational-statistical gaps for a variety of problems such as recovery in the multicommunity stochastic block model, sparse PCA, tensor PCA, the planted clique problem, certification in the zero-temperature Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model, the planted sparse vector problem, and for finding solutions in random
k
-SAT problems.

Furthermore, it was observed that the predictions from this method generally agree with those conjectured using other techniques (for example, statistical physics heuristics based on studying BP/AMP fixed points, see e.g.
[
6
,
7
,
17
]
). Some of the merits of the low-degree polynomial framework include that it is relatively easy to use (e.g. compared to proving SOS lower bounds), and that low degree polynomials capture the power of the “local algorithms” framework used in e.g.

[
9
,
5
]
as well as algorithms which incorporate global information, such as spectral methods or a constant number of iterations of Approximate Message Passing
[
25
]
.”

In this work, we continue to study the power of low-degree polynomials for the (average case) broadcast on trees problem.
In broadcast on trees the goal is to estimate the value of the Markov process at the root given its value at the leaves and the goal is to do
so for arbitrarily deep trees.
Two key parameters of the model are the arity of the tree
d
and the magnitude of the second eigenvalue
\lambda
of the broadcast chain.

A fundamental result in this area
[
13
]
is that when
d|\lambda|^{2}>1
nontrivial reconstruction of the root is possible just from knowing the counts of the leaves of different types, whereas when
d|\lambda|^{2}<1
such count statistics have no mutual information with the root (but more complex statistics of the leaves may)
[
18
,
24
]
. This threshold
d|\lambda|^{2}=1
is known as the
Kesten-Stigum threshold
. The Kesten-Stigum threshold plays a fundamental role in problems, such as algorithmic recovery in the stochastic block model
[
21
,
3
,
23
,
1
]
and phylogenetic reconstruction
[
19
]
. Count statistics can be viewed as degree 1 polynomials of the leaves, which begs the question of what information more general polynomials can extract from the leaves.
See
[
20
,
22
]
for surveys on the topic.

In
[
14
]
it was shown that
\lambda=0
even polynomials of degree
N^{c}
, where
N=d^{\ell}
is the number of leaves of for a
d
-ary tree of depth
\ell
, for a small
c>0
are not able to correlate with the root label (as
\ell
tends to
\infty
) whereas computationally efficient reconstruction is generally possible as long as
d
is a sufficiently large constant
[
18
]
.

The main motivation of
[
14
]
was to prove that low degree polynomials fail below the Kesten Stigum bound:
“It is natural to wonder if the Kesten-Stigum threshold
d|\lambda|^{2}=1
is sharp for low-degree polynomial reconstruction, analogous to how it is sharp for robust reconstruction.”
However the main result of
[
14
]
only established this in the very special case of
\lambda=0
.
This problem is also stated in the ICM 2022 paper and talk on the broadcast process
[
22
]
:
“
The authors of
[
14
]
ask if a similar phenomenon holds through the non-linear regime. For example,
is it true that polynomials of bounded degree have vanishing correlation with
X_{0}
in the regime where
d\lambda^{2}<1
?
”
The main results of this paper prove that this is indeed the case.
We proceed with formal definitions and statement of the main result.

1.1.
Definitions and Main Result

Let us begin with define the type of trees we will be investigating in this paper, which is a slight generalization of
d
-ary tree.

Definition 1.1
.

A rooted tree
T
with root
\rho
of depth
\ell
with degree dominated by
d\geq 1
with parameter
R\geq 1
is a tree with a root
\rho
such that for each node
u
in
T
,

\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}},\,|L_{k}(u)|\leq Rd^{k},

where
L_{k}(u)
is the set of
k
th descendants of
u
.
Further, let
L
denote the set of vertices on the
\ell
th layer.

With the above definition, a
d
-ary rooted tree is a tree
T
with degree dominated by
d\geq 1
with parameter
R=1
. For a typical realization of Galton-Watson Tree of Poisson type with average degree
d
and depth
\ell
, is a
tree with degree dominated by
d\geq 1
with parameter
R\simeq\log(\ell)
.

Consider a
q\times q
ergodic transition matrix
M
, where
q\geq 2
. Let
\lambda
represent the second largest absolute value among the eigenvalues of
M
. Additionally, we define the stationary distribution of
M
as
\pi
.

The broadcasting process
X=(X_{v})_{v\in T}
, with state space
[q]
and transition matrix
M
, can be formally described as follows: As we reveal the values layer by layer, when the value
X_{u}
is revealed, the value of
X_{v}
for any child node
v
of
u
is independently distributed according to
M
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\{X_{v}=t\,|\,X_{u}=s\}=M_{st}.

In other words, the values of the nodes in the tree
T
are updated according to the transition matrix
M
, where each node’s value depends only on its parent node’s value. A formal definition of the process is given below:

Definition 1.2

(Broadcasting Process on Tree)
.

Let
q\geq 2
be a positive integer. For any rooted tree
T
with root
\rho
and a
q\times q
ergodic transition matrix
M
, the broadcasting process
X=(X_{v})_{v\in T}
with state space
[q]
, according to transition matrix
M
with an initial distribution
\nu
, is a random process with joint distribution given by:

\displaystyle\forall x=(x_{v})_{v\in T}\in[q]^{T},\,\,\,\,{\mathbb{P}}\{X=x\}=\nu(x_{\rho})\prod_{(v,u)}M_{x_{u},x_{v}},

where the product is taken over all pairs
(v,u)
such that
v
is a child node of
u
. Further, we use
x_{L}
to denote

x_{L}=(x_{v})_{v\in L}\in[q]^{L}.

With the assumption
\nu=\pi
,
X_{v}\sim\pi
for every
v\in T
, as
(X_{v})_{v\in P}
for every downward path of
T
forms a Markov Chain with transition matrix
M
. Further, let us make a remark about the Markov property of the process.

Remark 1.3

(Markov Property)
.

The probability measure defines a
Markov Random Field
on tree
T
. This implies that for any three subsets
A,B,
and
C
of
T
, if every path from a node in
A
to a node in
C
passes through a node in
B
, then the random variables
X_{A}
and
X_{C}
are conditionally independent given
X_{B}
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
x\in[q]^{T}
. For
u\in T
, let
x_{\leq u}=(x_{v})_{v\leq u}
. For each subset
U\subseteq T
and
x\in[q]^{T}
, let
x_{U}=(x_{u})_{u\in U}
.

Let
f:[q]^{T}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
, and suppose
f
depends only on
x_{U}
. This we will often abbreviate by writing

\displaystyle f(x)=f(x_{U}).

The next definition is about the notion of degrees for functions
x_{L}=(x_{v})_{v\in L}
. This is the generalization of degree of a polynomial.

Definition 1.5

(Efron-Stein Degree)
.

A polynomial
f
with variables
x_{L}
has Efron-Stein degree at most
d
if it can be expressed as a sum of functions
f_{S}
, where
S\subseteq L
and
f_{S}
is a function of
x_{S}=(x_{v})_{v\in S}
, such that each
S
has size bounded by
d
.

Our main result in the paper is:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
T
be a rooted tree of depth
\ell
and degree dominated by
d\geq 1
with parameter
R
. Consider the boardcast process on
T
with a
q\times q
transition matrix
M
and
X_{\rho}\sim\pi
.
If
M
is irreducible and aperiodic and
d\lambda^{2}<1
, then there exists a constant
c>0
which depends on
M
and
1-d\lambda^{2}
such that the following holds:
For any function
f(x_{L})
of Efron-Stein degree
\leq c\frac{\ell}{1+\log(R)}
,
we have

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(\mathbb{E}\big[f(X_{L})\,\big|\,X_{\rho}\big])\leq(\max\{d\lambda^{2},\lambda\})^{\ell/4}{\rm Var}(f(X_{L})).

Follows from the theorem, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 1.7
.

With the same setting as in Theorem
1.6
, for any function
f(x_{L})
of Efron-Stein degree
\leq c\frac{\ell}{1+\log(R)}
, and any function
g(x_{\rho})
of the root value, we have

{\rm Cor}(f(X_{L}),g(X_{\rho}))\leq(\max\{d\lambda^{2},\lambda\})^{\ell/4}.

The corollary follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(f(X_{L})-\mathbb{E}[f(X_{L})])\cdot(g(X_{\rho})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{\rho})])\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbb{E}[f(X_{L})\,|\,X_{\rho}]-\mathbb{E}[f(X_{L})])\cdot(g(X_{\rho})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{\rho})])\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\rm Var}(\mathbb{E}\big[f(X_{L})\,\big|\,X_{\rho}\big])}\cdot\sqrt{{\rm Var}(g(X_{\rho}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\max\{d\lambda^{2},\lambda\})^{\ell/4}\sqrt{{\rm Var}(f(X_{L}))}\cdot\sqrt{{\rm Var}(g(X_{\rho}))}.

Indeed, the main result is optimal in the fractal sense (Theorem
1.15
).
The proof of the theorem is based on recursion on fractal capacity of functions.
Let us introduce the necessary definitions and notations to introduce both the fractal capacity
and the proof overview.

1.2.
Fractal Capacity and Proof Overview

For convenience we will refer to the set of vertices of the tree as
T
. Following the standard poset convention,
we define

\displaystyle v<u

for
v,u\in T
when
v
is a descendants of
u
. For any subset
S\subseteq L
, we define a notion of
branch decomposition

for
S
.

To provide a clearer illustration,
we establish a correspondence between the vertices of
T
and words of varying lengths from
0
to
\ell
,
with vertices at the
k
th layer represented as words of length
k
.
We denote the root
\rho
as the empty word
()
.
For each vertex
u
, represented by the word
(b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{k}),

we define
d_{u}
as the number of descendants of
u
,
and we identify the descendants of
u
as

(b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{k},i)
with
i\in[d_{u}]
.
Notice that
v
is a descendant of
u
is equivalent to
u
is a prefix of
v
.
For brevity, for each
u=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\in T
, for each
i\in[d_{u}]
, let

\displaystyle u_{i}:=(u,i)=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k},i).

For
I\subseteq[d_{u}]
, let

\displaystyle u_{I}=\{u_{i}\}_{i\in I}.

Furthermore, we define the parent function
\mathfrak{p}(u)=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k-1})
and the children function
\mathfrak{c}(u)=u_{[d_{u}]}
.

Definition 1.8
.

For a non-empty subset
S\subseteq L
,
we introduce the notation
\rho(S)
to represent the nearest common ancestor of the elements in
S
.
When
|S|>1
, we define the
branch decomposition
of
S
as follows: Let
I(S)=\{i\in[\rho(S)]\,:\,S_{i}\neq\emptyset\}
then the brach decomposition is:

\displaystyle S=\sqcup_{i\in I(S)}S_{i},

where
S_{i}=S\cap T_{\rho(S)_{i}}
for
i\in[d_{\rho(S)}]
.
We also called each
S_{i}
as a
branch part
of
S
.

The branching decomposition is a key concept in the proof,
and we define the
fractal capacity
according to the number of iterations to decompose
S
into singletons.

Definition 1.9
.

Let

{\mathcal{A}_{1}}:=\Big\{\{u\}\,:\,u\in L\Big\}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset,

be the collection of singletons of
L
. We say a subcollection
\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
is

closed under decomposition
with base
\mathcal{A}_{1}

if for every
S\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\mathcal{A}_{1}
, we have
S_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
for
i\in I(S)
where
I(S)
is the set of components of the branched decomposition in Definition
1.8
.

Definition 1.10
.

For any
\mathcal{A}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset

which is closed under decomposition with base
\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
let

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset

be a new subcollection defined according to the following rules:

For any
S\in{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
,
S\in\mathcal{B}
if and only if one of the following two conditions holds

(1)

S\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

(2)

S\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}

and
S_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
for those
i
with
S_{i}=S\cap T_{\rho(S)_{i}}\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 1.11
.

The collection
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})
described above contains
\mathcal{A}
. Also, it is
closed under decomposition
with base
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

Proof.

To show
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, it is sufficient to show
\mathcal{A}\backslash\mathcal{A}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.
For any
S\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\mathcal{A}_{1}
, because
\mathcal{A}
is closed under decomposition,
S_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
for
i\in I(S)
. Hence,
S\in\mathcal{B}
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{B}
.
Now, for
S\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{A}_{1}
, each
S_{i}
with
i\in I(S)
is contained in
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, which in turn implies
\mathcal{B}
is closed under decomposition.

∎

Now, we define recursively that

(1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A}_{k-1}),

for positive integer
k\geq 2
.

Clearly, from the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, if
S\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
, then for any
i\in I(S)
,
S_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{k-1}
.
In particular, for each branch part
S^{\prime}
of
S
,
\rho(S^{\prime})<\rho(S)
. Given that there are only
\ell
layers of the tree,
we conclude that every non-emptyset of
S\subseteq L
is in
\mathcal{A}_{\ell+1}
. Therefore, together with Lemma
1.11
, we have the following chain of subcollections:

\{\{u\}\,:\,u\in L\}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{\ell+1}={\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset.

Definition 1.12

(Fractal Capacity)
.

For any non-empty subset
S\subseteq L
, we define the
fractal capacity
of
S
as the smallest
k
such that
S\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
.

Next, we compare the notion of fractal capacity and the size of the set:

Lemma 1.13
.

\mathcal{A}_{k}
contains all subsets
S\subseteq L
with
|S|=k
. Further, in a
d
-ary tree of depth
\ell\geq k
, there exists
S\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
with
|S|=d^{k-1}
.

Proof.

Indeed, we will show that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
contains all non-empty subsets of
L
of size
\leq k
.
This can be proved by induction on
k
.
The base case with
k=1
follows from the definition
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}:=\big\{\{v\}\,:\,v\in L\big\}
.
Suppose the claim holds up to some positive integer
k
. Let
S\subseteq L\backslash\emptyset
of size
|S|\leq k+1
.
If
|S|\leq k
, then
S\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})={\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
. In the case
|S|=k+1\geq 2
, notice that
\rho(S)
is not a leave.
Consider the branch decomposition of
S
(See Definition
1.8
):

S=\sqcup_{i\in I(S)}S_{i}.

Because
|I(S)|>1
, for each
i\in I(S)
we have
|S_{i}|<|S|=k+1
. Therefore,
S_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
for
i\in I(S)
, which in turn implies
S\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})={\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
. Therefore, the claim follows.

Now, to show the second statement. For every node
w
, let
S_{w}=\{v\in L\,:\,v\leq w\}
.
Observe that if
w
is
k-1
layers above
L
, then
S_{w}
are the
(k-1)
th descendants of
w
, which has size
|S_{w}|=d^{k-1}
.

We
claim
that for
w
which are
k
layers above
L
, then
S_{w}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
. Let us prove the claim by induction.
First, it is clear that for
w\in L
,
S_{w}=\{w\}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. Suppose the statement holds up to
k
. Take any
w
which is
k
layer above
L
. Then, the branch decomposition of
S_{w}=\sqcup_{i\in[d]}S_{w_{i}}.
With each
w_{i}
is
k-1
layer above
L
, we have
S_{w_{i}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
. Hence,
S_{w}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
due to
{\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})
.

∎

Figure 1.
S\subseteq L
with fractal-capacity 4 and
|S|=5
.

Definition 1.14
.

Given a collection
\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
.
A function
f:[q]^{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is called a
{\mathcal{A}}
-polynomial
if we can express

f(x)=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{A}}}f_{S}(x_{S})

where each
f_{S}
is a function of
x_{S}=(x_{v})_{v\in S}
.
Correspondingly,
a function
f:[q]^{T}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
has
fractal capacity

\leq k
if it is a
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
-polynomial.

The main result of the paper in terms of the fractal capacity is the following:

Theorem 1.15
.

With the same setting as in Theorem
1.6
,
there exists a constant
c>0
which depends on
M
and
1-d\lambda^{2}
such that the following holds:
For any function
f(x_{L})
with
fractal capacity

\leq c\frac{\ell}{1+\log(R)}
,
we have

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(\mathbb{E}\big[f(X_{L})\,\big|\,X_{\rho}\big])\leq(\max\{d\lambda^{2},\lambda\})^{\ell/4}{\rm Var}(f(X_{L})).

Indeed, Theorem
1.6
is a direct consequence of Theorem
1.15
, as
\mathcal{A}_{k}
-polynomials contains all polynomial of Efron-Stein degree
\leq k
.

Sketch Proof Idea:
For illustration, let us consider the case where
T
is a
2
-ary tree
with
M=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1+\lambda}{2}&\frac{1-\lambda}{2}\\
\frac{1-\lambda}{2}&\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\end{bmatrix}
, such matrix eigenvalue
\lambda
and
1
.
If
f
is a degree-
1
polynomial, we can express it in the form

f(x_{L})=\sum_{u\in L}f_{u}(x_{u}),

where each
f_{u}
is a function of
x_{u}
. Given our focus on the variance, without lose of generality, we assume
\mathbb{E}f_{u}(X_{u})=0
for each
u\in L
.
Then, our goal is to prove

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbb{E}[f(X_{L})\,|\,X_{\rho}]\big)^{2}\big]

is negligible comparing to
\mathbb{E}\big[(f(X_{L}))^{2}\big]
.
Following from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

(\sum_{i\in[k]}a_{i})^{2}=(\sum_{i\in[k]}1\cdot a_{i})^{2}\leq k\sum_{i\in[k]}a_{i}^{2},

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbb{E}[f(X_{L})\,|\,X_{\rho}]\big)^{2}\big]\leq

\displaystyle|L|\sum_{u\in L}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbb{E}[f_{u}(X_{u})\,|\,X_{\rho}]\big)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\ell}\sum_{u\in L}\lambda^{2\ell}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}\big]\lesssim(2\lambda^{2})^{\ell}\sum_{u\in L}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}\big],

where the second inequality is derived from the variance decay property of in a Markov Chain.
Then, if we can establish
\sum_{u\in L}\mathbb{E}\big[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}\big]

is at the same order as
\mathbb{E}\big[(f(X_{L}))^{2}\big]
, the proof is complete.

This scenario is achievable if, for most pairs of
u
and
v
within
L
, the correlation between
f_{u}(X_{u})
and
f_{v}(X_{v})
is sufficiently small, which is the case for degree-1 polynomials.

Now, let us take a closer look. Fix any two vertices
u
and
v
in
L
, with
w
as their nearest common ancestor. Suppose
w
is
k
layer above
L
, and
u\leq w_{1}
and
v\leq w_{2}
.
Let
\tilde{X}=(X_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\not\leq w_{2}}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f_{u}(X_{u})f_{v}(X_{v})]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f_{u}(X_{u})\mathbb{E}[f_{v}(X_{v})\,|\,\tilde{X}]]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[f_{v}(X_{v})\,|\,\tilde{X}])^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[(f_{v}(X_{v}))^{2}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[(f_{u}(X_{u}))^{2}]\cdot\lambda^{2k}\mathbb{E}[(f_{v}(X_{v}))^{2}],

where the last inequality again follows from the variance decay of a Markov Chain.
The above inequality implies that the correlation between
f_{u}(X_{u})
and
f_{v}(X_{v})
is at most
\lambda^{2k}
.

Our proof of the main theorem tries to generalize the argument above to low degree polynomials using the following ideas:

I. Bounding Covariance:

Suppose
f_{\alpha}
and
f_{\beta}
are two functions such that

(1)

f_{\alpha}(x_{S})
with that
S\subseteq L
satisfying
S\cap\{v^{\prime}\,:\,v^{\prime}\leq w_{2}\}\neq\emptyset
.

(2)

f_{\beta}(x_{S^{\prime}})
is any function such that we know

\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[f_{\beta}(X_{S^{\prime}})\,|\,\tilde{X}])^{2}]\ll(\mathbb{E}(f_{\beta}(X_{S^{\prime}}))^{2},

where we use
a\ll b
to indicate
a
is much smaller than
b
, keeping it not precise to avoid distraction from technicality.

Then, we have
\mathbb{E}f_{\alpha}(X_{\alpha})f_{\beta}(X_{\beta})\ll\mathbb{E}[(f_{\alpha}(X_{\alpha}))^{2}]\mathbb{E}[(f_{\beta}(X_{\beta}))^{2}]
.

II. Choosing a good decomposition of the function:

In essence, our proof strategy for any given function
f(x_{L})

revolves around decomposing
f(x_{L})
into a sum of functions
f_{\alpha}(x)
for
\alpha
in some index set
I
,
such that

(1)

|I|\lesssim d^{\ell}
,

(2)

For each
\alpha
,
\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[f_{\alpha}(x)\,|\,X_{\rho})^{2}]\ll\mathbb{E}[(f_{\alpha}(x))^{2}]
,

(3)

Whenever
\alpha\neq\beta
, we can find
w\in T
so that
f_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha})
and
f_{\beta}(x_{\beta})
satisfies the covariance bound in I. (Possibly with a switch of the roles of
\alpha
and
\beta
).

If this is the case, then we can follow the argument in the degree 1 case to show that desired result.
The proof of the main theorem builds on this strategy, advancing through a recursion on the fractal capacity of the function. This recursive approach relies on the following property:

III. From functions to their products:

Suppose we have two functions
f_{\alpha}
and
f_{\beta}
such that
f_{\alpha}
is a function with variable
(x_{v})_{v\in L\,:\,v\leq w_{1}}

and
f_{\beta}
is a function with variable
(x_{v})_{v\in L\,:\,v\leq w_{2}}
. Further, if

\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[f_{\alpha}(X)\,|\,\tilde{X}])^{2}]\ll\mathbb{E}[(f_{\alpha}(X))^{2}]

and

\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[f_{\beta}(X)\,|\,\tilde{X}])^{2}]\ll\mathbb{E}[(f_{\beta}(X))^{2}],

where
\tilde{X}=(X_{v})_{v\not\leq w}
.
Then,
g(x)=f_{\alpha}(x)f_{\beta}(x)
also satisfies

\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[g(X)\,|\,\tilde{X}])^{2}]\ll\mathbb{E}[(g(X))^{2}].

Although proving III. requires some effort, its essence mirrors that of Property I, employing the Markov property and leveraging the variance decay characteristic of the functions involved. Effectively, Property III enables the derivation of additional functions exhibiting variance decay, which allows us to derive the main result in an iterative formulation.

The main theorem essentially are carried out following the three key ideas I-III. The technical difficulty comes from finding the suitable decomposition which has the right variance decay property that is presented in the Kesten-Stigum Bound.

•

In Section
2
, we give additional notations and basic tools.

•

In Section
3
, we formulate the main theorem we want to prove as an induction statement.

•

In Section
4
, we discuss the case for degree 1 polynomial, which prove the base case of the induction in the theorem, and the results for degree 1 polynomial will be used in the inductive step.

•

In Section
5
, we give a procedure to decompose
{\mathcal{B}}
-polynomial
f
for a given collection
{\mathcal{B}}
.

•

In Section
6
and
7
, we derive the proof of Theorem
3.6
, the inductive step for proving Theorem
3.1
.

•

In Section
8
and
9
, we derive the main result in the general case.

•

In Appendix
A
, we provide a proof of Proposition
4.3
, which is one technical obstacle for getting our main result from Theorem
3.1
to the general setting (Theorem
1.6
). It is postponed to this section due to the proof is essentially a result about Markov Chain.

•

In Appendix
B
, we provided some standard result for decay of Markov Chain.

2.
Additional Notations and Tools

For any positive integer
n
, let
[n]
denote the set of positive integers from 1 to
n
, inclusive:
[n]={1,2,\dots,n}
.
For integers
a
and
b
where
a<b
, let
[a,b]=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
.

We introduce the notation for the height of a node
u

and we define:

T_{u}:=T\cap\{v\in T\,:\,v\leq u\}\mbox{ and }L_{u}=T_{u}\cap L.

It is worth noting that
T_{u}
can be seen as a rooted tree with
{\rm h}(u)
layers, having
u
as its root and
L_{u}
as its set of leaves.

For any
u\in T
and
k\in[{\rm h}(u)]
, let
L_{k}(u)
be the set of
k
th descendants of
u
, i.e., those descendants that are
k
levels down.
For brevity, let
L_{k}:=L_{k}(\rho)
and
L=L_{\ell}(\rho)
, where
\ell
is the depth of the tree.

Further, for any
0\leq k\leq{\rm h}(u)
,

(2)

\displaystyle D_{k}(u)=\{v\in T\,:\,v\leq u\,\mbox{ and }{\rm h}(v)=k\}.

denote the set of vertices which are
k
-th descendants of
u
. Note that
D_{k}(u)=L_{{\rm h}(u)-k}(u)
.

For a given collection of of subsets
\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
,
we define the following subcollections: For each
u\in T
, let

{\mathcal{A}}_{u}:=\{S\in{\mathcal{A}}\,:\,\rho(S)=u\}
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq u}:=\{S\in{\mathcal{A}}\,:\,\rho(S)\leq u\}
, and

{\mathcal{A}}_{<u}:=\{S\in{\mathcal{A}}\,:\,\rho(S)<u\}
.

Definition 2.1
.

[Conditional Expectation]
For each
U\subseteq T
and let
f:[q]^{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}
let

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{U}f)(x):=\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X)\,\Big|\,X_{v}=x_{v}\,:\,v\not\in\bigcup_{u\in U}\{w\in T\,:\,w<u\}\Big].

To rephrase it, the function
(\mathbb{E}_{U}f)(x)
represents the expected value of
f(X)

condition on
X_{v}=x_{v}
for all vertices
v
that are not descendents of any
u\in U
.
Clearly,

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{U}f)(X)=\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X)\,\Big|\,X_{v}\,:\,v\not\in\bigcup_{u\in U}\{w\in T\,:\,w<u\}\Big].

Furthermore, we will abuse the notation and denote
\mathbb{E}_{u}
as
\mathbb{E}_{\{u\}}
for
u\in T
, as it will be used repeatedly.
Finally, for any
k\in[0,\ell]
, we set

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{k}f)(x):=\mathbb{E}\Big[f(X)\,\Big|\,\forall v\in T\,\mbox{ with }{\rm h}(v)\geq k,\,X_{v}=x_{v}\Big].

If
f
is a function of
x_{L}
, then by the
Markov Property
we have
(\mathbb{E}_{k}f)(x)
is a function of
(x_{v}\,:\,v\in D_{k}(\rho))
.

The following lemma is a well-known statement from Markov-Chain.
Let us formulate it using the Broadcast Process.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
M
is irreducible and aperiodic, then there exists
C=C(M)>1
so that the following hold:
For any
u\in T
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that
{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}(u)
exists.
For every function
a
with input
x_{u}
,

(3)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\Big[(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}(u)}a)(X_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}(u)})\Big]\leq Ck^{2q}\lambda^{2k}{\rm Var}\big[a(X_{u})\big]

and

(4)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left(\max_{\theta\in[q]}\big|a(\theta)-\mathbb{E}a(X_{u})\big|\right)^{2}\leq{\rm Var}_{Y\sim\pi}a(Y)\leq C\left(\max_{\theta\in[q]}\big|a(\theta)-\mathbb{E}a(X_{u})\big|\right)^{2}.

And from the above two inequalities,
adjusting the constant
C
if necessary, we also have

(5)

\displaystyle\max_{\theta\in[q]}\big|(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}(u)}a)(\theta)-\mathbb{E}a(X_{u})\big|\leq Ck^{q}\lambda^{k}\max_{\theta\in[q]}\big|a(\theta)-\mathbb{E}a(X_{u})\big|.

We include a proof of this lemma in Section
B
.

2.1.
Basis of functions on
[q]^{L}

In this section, we will fix a basis for the space of functions from
[q]
to
\mathbb{R}
for a Markov chain
M
.

Definition 2.3
.

For a given
q\times q
ergodic and irreducible transition matrix
M
,
we
fix
a basis
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in[0,q-1]}
for the space of functions from
[q]
to
\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi_{0}
is the constant function
1
and
\phi_{i}
for
i\in[q-1]
are functions such that

\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\pi}\phi_{i}(Y)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\pi}\phi_{i}^{2}(Y)=1.

Definition 2.4
.

For a given
\ell
layer rooted tree
T
and
q\times q
transition matrix described in Lemma
2.2
, for
\sigma\in[0,q-1]^{L}
, let

\phi_{\sigma}(x)=\prod_{v\in L}\phi_{\sigma(v)}(x_{v}).

We write
S(\sigma)=\{v:\sigma(v)\neq 0\}
and

|\sigma|=|S(\sigma)|
.

Remark 2.5
.

We remark that
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
is a function with variables in
(x_{v}\,:\,S(\sigma))
.

The fact that
\phi_{0},\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{q-1}
forms a basis implies that:

Lemma 2.6
.

Every function
f:[q]^{U}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
can be expressed uniquely in the form

(6)

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{\sigma\,:\,S(\sigma)\subseteq U}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x).

Remark 2.7
.

With the above representation, if
f
is not a constant function, the
Efron-Stein degree
of
f
equals to the largest magnitude of
|\sigma|
among those
\sigma
such that
c_{\sigma}\neq 0
.

Definition 2.8
.

Given a tree
T
and a
q\times q
ergodic transition matrix
M
,
let
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in[q-1]}
be the functions described in Lemma
2.2
.
For a collection of subsets
\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
, let

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A}):=\{\sigma\in[0,q-1]^{L}\,:\,S(\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}\}.

For any
u\in T\backslash L
, and
\sigma\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{A}}_{u})
, let
S=S(\sigma)
and

(8)

\displaystyle\psi_{\sigma}(x):=\prod_{i\in I(S)}\tilde{\phi}_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x),

where
P_{{i}}\sigma(x)_{j}=\sigma(x)_{j}1(j\in S_{i})
and

(9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x):=\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x)-\mathbb{E}\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X)

for any
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}({\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset)
.
For simplicity, let us also denote

I(\sigma):=I(S(\sigma)),\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\rho(\sigma):=\rho(S(\sigma)).

3.
The overall inductive argument

Here we present the version of the theorem with additional assumption on the transition matrix
M
that

(10)

\displaystyle c_{M}:=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0.

Theorem 3.1
.

Given the rooted tree
T
and the transition matrix
M
described in Theorem
1.6
, and under
the additional assumption that
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
, there exists
c>0
dependent on

M
and
d
(and implicitly on
c_{M}
as well) so that the following holds:
For any function
f
of the leaves with
fractal capacity

\leq c\frac{\ell}{\log(dR)}
,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(\mathbb{E}\big[f(X)\,\big|\,X_{\rho}\big])\leq(d\lambda^{2})^{\ell/4}{\rm Var}(f(X)).

We will first derive the version mentioned above, as it substantially reduces the technical complexity without compromising the structural integrity of the proof in the general setting where
c_{M}
might be
0
.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
will be carried out by induction on
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
-polynomials.
Let us introduce the necessary notations to outline this induction process.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be the constant such that

\displaystyle\max\{d\lambda^{2},\lambda\}=\exp(-1.1\varepsilon).

The constant
\varepsilon
is introduced to improve the readability of the paper.
Intuitively, we aim to define
d\lambda^{2}=\exp(-\varepsilon)
,
but we relax this definition slightly so that
inequalities like the following hold when
\ell
is sufficiently large:

\text{poly}(\ell)(d\lambda^{2})^{\ell}\leq\exp(-\varepsilon\ell).

Assumption 3.3
.

We say that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies assumption
3.3
with parameters
({\rm h^{*}},c^{*})
where
{\rm h^{*}}>0
and
0<c^{*}<1
, if

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{T}\backslash\emptyset

is
closed under decomposition
, and morever,

(1)

For any
v\in T
with
{\rm h}(v)\geq{\rm h^{*}}
and a

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq v}
-polynomial
f
,

(11)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)(X)\big]\leq\exp\big(-\varepsilon({\rm h}(v)-{\rm h^{*}}\big)){\rm Var}\big[f(X)\big].

(2)

For any
v\in T
with
{\rm h}(v)\geq{\rm h^{*}}
and a

{\mathcal{A}}_{\leq v}
-polynomial
f
with
\mathbb{E}f(X)=0
,

(12)

\displaystyle c^{*}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)^{2}(X_{v})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)^{2}(X_{v})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(v)}=\theta]\leq\frac{1}{c^{*}}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)^{2}(X_{v})\right],

for all
\theta\in[q]
.

The inequality (
11
) bears a resemblance to the inequality we aim to prove in Theorem
3.1
. The second inequality, (
12
), will later be seen as a crucial step proving the inductive phase of our proof. Indeed, in the case where
c_{M}>0
, the condition (
12
) can be easily satisfied by appropriately choosing
c^{*}
:

Lemma 3.4
.

For any given
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{T}\backslash\emptyset
which is closed under decomposition. If it satisfies (
11
) with a given parameter
{\rm h^{*}}
and
c_{M}>0
,
then
{\mathcal{A}}
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with parameter
{\rm h^{*}}
and

c^{*}:=\min\Big\{c_{M},\frac{1}{\min_{j}\pi(j)}\Big\}>0.

In other words, we can choose
c^{*}
with
no dependence on either

{\rm h^{*}}

or

{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary function
f
with variables
(x_{u}\,:\,u\leq v)
for some
v\in T\backslash\{\rho\}
.

Let
g(x):=(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)^{2}(x)
. By the
Markov Property
,
(\mathbb{E}_{v}f)(x)
is a function of
x_{v}
, which in turn implies
g(x)=g(x_{v})
.
Now, for any
\theta\in[q]
, fix an index
j_{0}\in[q]
such that
g(j_{0})\geq\mathbb{E}g(X_{v})
.
Relying on
g
is a non-negative function,

\mathbb{E}[g(X_{v})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(v)}=\theta]=\sum_{j\in[q]}M_{\theta j}g(j)\geq M_{\theta j_{0}}g(j_{0})\geq c_{M}\mathbb{E}g(X_{j}).

By unraveling the definition of
g
, we can satisfy the first inequality of (
12
) as long as
c^{*}<c_{M}
. The proof for the second
inequality follows a similar logic, using the condition

c^{*}\leq\frac{1}{\min_{j}\pi(j)}
and the trival inequality
\max_{i,j}M_{ij}\leq 1
.
∎

Given this notation, the proof of Theorem
3.1
proceeds by induction, with the base case and inductive articulated in the subsequent two statements.

Proposition 3.5
.

Given the rooted tree
T
and the transition matrix
M
described in Theorem
1.6
, and under the additional assumption that
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
.
There exists
C=C(M,\varepsilon)\geq 1
so that the following holds:

Fix
\rho^{\prime}\in T
and
0\leq m\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
,
if
f(x)
is a degree 1 polynomials of variables
(x_{v}\,:\,v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime}))
, then

(13)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}f)(X)\big]\leq\exp\Big(-\varepsilon\big({\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})-m-C(\log(R)+1)\big)\Big){\rm Var}\big[f(X)\big].

Theorem 3.6
.

Given the rooted tree
T
and the transition matrix
M
described in Theorem
1.6
, and under the additional assumption that
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a collection of subsets satisfying Assumption
3.3
with parameters
({\rm h^{*}},\,c^{*})
.
Then, there exists
C=C(M,d,c^{*})\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with parameters
\big({\rm h^{*}}+C(\log(R)+1),\,c^{*}\big)
.

Let us derive the proof of Theorem
3.1
based on the above two statements.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

First, we
claim
that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
contains all non-empty subsets of
L
of size
\leq k
.
This can be proved by induction on
k
.
The base case with
k=1
follows from the definition
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}:=\big\{\{v\}\,:\,v\in L\big\}
.
Suppose the claim holds for some positive integer
k
. Let
S\subseteq L\backslash\emptyset
with
|S|\leq k+1
.
If
|S|\leq k
, then
S\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})={\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
. In the case where
|S|=k+1\geq 2
, notice that
\rho(S)
is not a leave.
Consider the branch decomposition of
S
(See Definition
1.8
):

S=\sqcup_{i\in I(S)}S_{i}.

Because
|I(S)|>1
, for each
i\in I(S)
we have
|S_{i}|<|S|=k+1
. Therefore,
S_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
for
i\in I(S)
, which in turn implies
S\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})={\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
. Therefore, the claim follows.

Second, we apply Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
3.4
to get
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with parameter
{\rm h^{*}}=C_{\ref{prop: inductionBaseSimple}}(\log(R)+1)
, where
C_{\ref{prop: inductionBaseSimple}}=C(M,d)
is the constant introduced in the Proposition and

c^{*}=\min\Big\{c_{M},\frac{1}{\min_{j}\pi(j)}\Big\}>0.

Then, by applying Theorem
3.6
inductively on the chain
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
, we can conclude that
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with parameter
{\rm h^{*}}=C(\log(R)+1)k
and the same
c^{*}
described above, provided that
C=C(M,d,c^{*})
is the maximum of the constants
C
described in Proposition
3.5

and Theorem
3.6
.
In other words, for any
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
-polynomial
f
,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho}f)(X)\big]\leq\exp\big(-\varepsilon(\ell-C(\log(R)+1)k)\big){\rm Var}\big[f(X)\big].

The theorem follows by choosing
k=\frac{1}{2C(\log(R)+1)}\ell
.

∎

4.
Variance Decomposition and Variance Estimate for degree 1 polynomials

To describe the goal of this section, let us begin with the variance decomposition of degree 1 polynomials in a slight generalized form. Essentially, the following statement is a direct consequence of the conditional variance formula. However, for the sake of completeness, a detailed proof is provided below.

Lemma 4.1
.

Fix
\rho^{\prime}\in T
and
0\leq k\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
, consider a function

g:[q]^{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of the form

g(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}g_{v}(x)\quad\mbox{where}\quad g_{v}(x)=g_{v}(x_{\leq v}).

Then,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]=

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}g)(X_{\rho^{\prime}})\big]+\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}:{\rm h}(w)\geq k}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}g_{w})(X_{w})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(w)}\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[g_{v}(X)\,\big|\,X_{v}\big],

where for
w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}
with
{\rm h}(w)\geq k+1
,

g_{w}(x):=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(w)}g_{v}(x).

Our goal is to show that when
d\lambda^{2}<1
,
{\rm Var}[g(X)]
is of the same order as
\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X)]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose the transition matrix
M
satisfies
d\lambda^{2}<1
and the tree
T
has growth factor
R
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(M,\varepsilon)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
Let
\rho^{\prime}\in T
,
l^{\prime}:={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
, and
k\in[0,l^{\prime}]
.
Consider a function of the form
g(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}g_{v}(x_{v})
. Then,

(14)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]\leq CR\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X_{v})].

The opposite bound does not depend on
d\lambda^{2}\leq 1
. However, the proof in the general case where
c_{M}=0
is not straight-forward. We state it in full generality but will defer the general proof and prove it here in the simpler case where
c_{M}>0
.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists a constant
C=C(M,d)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
Let
\rho^{\prime}\in T
,
l^{\prime}:={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
, and
k\in[0,l^{\prime}]
.
For any degree-1 funcion
g
with variables
(x_{v}\,:\,v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime}))
.
There exists functions
g_{v}(x)=g_{v}(x_{v})
for
v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
so that the following holds:

(1)

g(X)=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}g_{v}(X_{u})
almost surely. (They may not agree as functions from
[q]^{T}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
.)

(2)

For any
u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq k
,

(15)

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{k}(u)}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X_{v})]\leq CR^{3}{\rm Var}\big[\sum_{v\in D_{k}(u)}g_{v}(X_{v})\big].

In particular, taking
u=\rho^{\prime}
we have

(16)

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X_{v})]\leq CR^{3}{\rm Var}[g(X)].

We postpone the proof of Proposition in full generality in Appendix
A
,
due to the technical complexity of the proof and the fact that the proof is about properties of a Markov Chain. Instead, a statement of the proposition and its proof in the case where
c_{M}>0
is provided in this section.

Now, the purpose of this section is twofold.

•

First, it is the derivation of the variance related estimates: Lemma
4.1
, Lemma
4.2
, and Proposition
4.3
with the additional assumption that
c_{M}>0
. Additionally, we summarise the estimates into a single statements, as stated in Lemma
4.7
.

•

Second, it is the derivation of the base case of the induction, Proposition
3.5
.

4.1.
Variance Decomposition and Estimates

Before we proceed to the proof of Lemma
4.1
, let us remark on the following consequence of the lemma.

Remark 4.4
.

For any
g
described in Lemma
4.1
,
if we define
h(x):=(\mathbb{E}_{k}g)(x)
and
h_{v}(x):=(\mathbb{E}_{v}g_{v})(x_{v})
,
then by applying the lemma to
g
and to
h
, we conclude that

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]={\rm Var}[(\mathbb{E}_{k}g)(X)]+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[g_{v}(X)\,\big|\,X_{v}\big].

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

First, for
u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq k
,

\tilde{g}_{v}(x):=g_{v}(x)-\mathbb{E}g_{v}(X).

Notice the following holds:

\tilde{g}_{u}(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(u)}\tilde{g}_{v}(x).

Let us start decomposing the variance of
g
.

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]=

\displaystyle\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}(X)\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})\,:\,\rho(v,v^{\prime})=w}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}(X)\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}}(X)\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})\,:\,\rho(v,v^{\prime})=w}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\Big(\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(w)}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})\big]-\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(w)\,:\,\rho(v,v^{\prime})<w}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})\big]\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big].

Notice that for
w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}
with
{\rm h}(w)>k
,

\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(w)}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})=\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)\big]

and

\displaystyle\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(w)\,:\,\rho(v,v^{\prime})<w}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})=

\displaystyle\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{c}}(w)}\sum_{v,v^{\prime}\in D_{k}(w^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v^{\prime}})(X_{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{c}}(w)}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w^{\prime}})^{2}(X)\big].

Hence,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)\big]-\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{c}}(w)}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w^{\prime}})^{2}(X)\big]\Big)+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)\big]-\sum_{w^{\prime}\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}:{\rm h}(w^{\prime})\geq k}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathfrak{p}}(w)}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{g}_{\rho^{\prime}})^{2}(X)\big]+\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}:{\rm h}(w)>k}\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)-(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathfrak{p}}(w)}\tilde{g}_{w})^{2}(X)\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{g}_{v}^{2}(X)\big]\underbrace{-\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v})^{2}(X)\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{v}\tilde{g}_{v})^{2}(X)\big]}_{=0}-\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{{\mathfrak{p}}(v)}\tilde{g}_{v})^{2}(X)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{g}_{\rho^{\prime}})^{2}(X)\big]+\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}:{\rm h}(w)\geq k}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}g_{w})(X_{w})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(w)}\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[g_{v}(X)\,\big|\,X_{v}\big].

∎

Next, let us show the proof of Lemma
4.2
.
This follows the standard second moment calculation for the tree model where it is shown that covariance terms decay exponentially in the distance between the corresponding function on the tree.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Let
C_{0}=C_{0}(M,d)
denote the constant introduced in the statement of the Lemma. Its precise value will be determined along the proof. Without lose of generality, we may assume both
\mathbb{E}g(X)=0
and
\mathbb{E}g_{v}(X_{v})=0
for
v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
, and the variance of each function is simply its the second moment.

For brevity, let
\sigma:=(\mathbb{E}(g_{u}(X))^{2})^{1/2}
for
u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
.
By (
3
) from Lemma
2.2
,
for
s\in[l^{\prime}-k]
,

(17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)}g_{u})^{2}(X_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)})\big]\leq C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}\sigma_{u}^{2},

where
C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}\geq 1
is the constant introduced in the Lemma.

In particular, if
\rho(u,u^{\prime})=\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)
for
u,u^{\prime}\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
, then

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}g_{u}(X)g_{u^{\prime}}(X)|=

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)}g_{u}g_{u^{\prime}})(X_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)})\big]\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)}g_{u})^{2}(X_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)})\big]\big)^{1/2}\cdot\big(\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)}g_{u^{\prime}})^{2}(X_{\mathfrak{p}^{s}(u)})\big]\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}\sigma_{u}\sigma_{u^{\prime}}.

For each
s\in[l^{\prime}-k]
and
v\in D_{k+s}(\rho^{\prime})
,
let
\sigma_{v}:=\sum_{u\in L_{s}(v)}\sigma_{u}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(g(X))^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}|\mathbb{E}g_{u}(X)g_{u^{\prime}}(X)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in[l^{\prime}-k]}\sum_{v\in D_{k+s}(\rho^{\prime})}\sum_{u,u^{\prime}}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}\sigma_{u}\sigma_{u^{\prime}},

where the sum
\sum_{u,u^{\prime}}
is taken over all ordered pairs
(u,u^{\prime})

with
u,u^{\prime}\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(v)
and the nearest common ancestor of
u
and
u^{\prime}
is
v
.
By relaxing the constraint of the summation we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(g(X))^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in[l^{\prime}-k]}\sum_{v\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(\rho^{\prime})}\sum_{u,u^{\prime}\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(v)}Cs^{2q}\lambda^{2s}\sigma_{u}\sigma_{u^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in[l^{\prime}-k]}\sum_{v\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(\rho^{\prime})}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}\Big(\sum_{u\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(v)}\sigma_{u}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in[l^{\prime}-k]}\sum_{v\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(\rho^{\prime})}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}Rd^{s}\sum_{u\in L_{l^{\prime}-k+s}(v)}\sigma_{u}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{s\in[l^{\prime}-k]}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}Rd^{s}\Big)\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\sigma_{u}^{2}.

Next,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\lambda^{2s}Rd^{s}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}RC_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}s^{2q}\exp(-1.1\varepsilon s):=C_{0}R.

Hence,
C_{0}
depends on
\varepsilon
,
q
, and
C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}
. It is a constant which is determined by
M
and
\varepsilon
.

∎

Let us formulate Proposition
4.3
under the additional assumption that
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
.
Indeed, in this case, the bound does not depend on
R
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose the transition matrix
M
satisfies
c_{M}>0
.
There exists a constant
C=C(M,\varepsilon)\geq 1
so that the following holds:
Let
\rho^{\prime}\in T
,
l^{\prime}:={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
, and
k\in[0,l^{\prime}]
.
For any function

g=[q]^{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of the form

g(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}g_{v}(x_{v}).

The following holds:
For any
u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq k
,

(18)

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{k}(u)}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X_{v})]\leq C{\rm Var}\big[\sum_{v\in D_{k}(u)}g_{v}(X_{v})\big].

In particular, taking
u=\rho^{\prime}
we have

(19)

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rm Var}[g_{v}(X_{v})]\leq C{\rm Var}[g(X)].

The proof of the Proposition relies on the following immediate consequence of
c_{M}>0
:

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose
M
is a
q\times q
ergodic transition matrix with
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
.
There exists
C=C(M)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
For any
u\in T\backslash\{\rho\}
and a function
h(x)=h(x_{u})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}]\geq\frac{1}{C(M)}{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})].

For completeness, we provide the proof in the Appendix.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

We adapt the notation from Lemma
4.1
. For
w\leq\rho^{\prime}
with
{\rm h}(w)>k
, let

g_{w}(x):=\sum_{u\in D_{k}(w)}g_{u}(x).

Now, we apply Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.6
to get

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[g(X)]=

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}g_{\rho^{\prime}})(X_{\rho^{\prime}})\big]+\sum_{w\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\backslash\{\rho^{\prime}\}:{\rm h}(w)\geq k}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{w}g_{w})(X_{w})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(w)}\big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[g_{v}(X)\,\big|\,X_{v}\big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u}g_{u})(X_{u})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ref{lem: MCvarDecaySimple}}}\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rm Var}[g_{u}(X_{u})],

where we used the fact that all the terms are non-negative, the first inequality is obtained by looking at the second terms for the summands with
h(w)=k
and

C_{\ref{lem: MCvarDecaySimple}}
is the
M
-dependent constant introduced in Lemma
4.6
.

∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose
d\lambda^{2}<1
and the growth factor is at most
R
.
There exists a constant
C=C(M,d)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
Fix
\rho^{\prime}\in T
and
0\leq m\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
,
if
f_{m}(x)
is a function in the form

f_{m}(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}f_{v}(x_{\leq v}).

with

\mathbb{E}f_{m}(X)=0.

Then, there exists
\bar{f}_{v}(x_{\leq v})
for
v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})
such that
their sum
\bar{f}_{m}(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}\bar{f}_{v}(x_{\leq v})
satisfies

\bar{f}_{m}(X)=f_{m}(X)\mbox{ almost surely,}

and for
u\leq\rho^{\prime}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq m
,

(20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{CR^{3}}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}\bar{f}^{2}_{v}(X)\leq\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\bar{f}_{v}(X)\Big)^{2}\leq CR\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}\bar{f}^{2}_{v}(X).

The statement of the lemma using
\bar{f}_{m}
and

\bar{f}_{b}
covers also the case
c_{M}=0
. We will prove the Lemma by using either Proposition
4.3
or Proposition
4.5
with the assumption
c_{M}>0
. In the later case, it suffice to simply take
f_{v}(x_{\leq v})=\bar{f}_{v}(x_{\leq v})
.

Remark 4.8
.

Note that the lemma implies the following:
For any
u\leq\rho^{\prime}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq m
,
let

(21)

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{m,u}(x):=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\bar{f}_{v}(x).

Then, for any given
m\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
and
u\in D_{k^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})
,
we have

\mathbb{E}\bar{f}_{m,u}^{2}(X)\leq CR\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}\bar{f}^{2}_{v}(X)=CR\sum_{w\in D_{k}(u)}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(w)}\mathbb{E}\bar{f}^{2}_{v}(X)\leq C^{2}R^{4}\sum_{w\in D_{k}(u)}\bar{f}_{m,w}^{2}(X),

where the first inequality follows from the second inequality of (
20
) and the second inequality follows from the first inequality of (
20
).

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Let

h(x):=(\mathbb{E}_{m}f_{m})(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}(\mathbb{E}_{v}f_{v})(x_{v}).

Note that
h

is a degree one function of the variables
(x_{v}\,:\,v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime}))
. Thus, we could apply
Proposition
4.3
to show the existence of 1-variable functions
h_{v}(x_{v})

for
v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})
such that

(22)

\displaystyle h(X)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}h_{v}(X_{v})\mbox{\,\,\,\, almost surely }

and for any
u\in T(\rho^{\prime})
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq m
,

(23)

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}{\rm Var}[h_{v}(X_{v})]\leq C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}{\rm Var}\big[\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}h_{v}(X_{v})\big],

where
C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}\geq 1
is the constant introduced in Proposition
4.3
.

Since
\mathbb{E}h(X)=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{m}f_{m})(X)=0
, we may also assume that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}h_{v}(X)=0

for
v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})
, as a constant shift of the functions will not affect (
22
) and (
23
). Now, consider the following functions: For
v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})
, let

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{v}(x_{\leq v})=f_{v}(x_{\leq v})-\mathbb{E}f_{v}(X_{\leq v})+h_{v}(x_{v})

and

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{m}(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}\bar{f}_{v}(x).

First, since
\bar{f}_{v}(x_{\leq v})
is defined as the sum of three terms with mean
0
,
we have
\mathbb{E}\bar{f}_{v}(X_{\leq v})=0
.

Second,

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{m}(X)=

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}\Big(f_{v}(X_{\leq v})-(\mathbb{E}_{v}f_{v})(X_{v})+h_{v}(X_{v})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{m}(X)-h(X)+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}h_{v}(X_{v})

\displaystyle\underset{\mbox{a.s.}}{=}

\displaystyle f_{m}(X).

By Remark
4.4
,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\bar{f}_{v}(X)\big]=

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\Big[\Big(\mathbb{E}_{m}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\bar{f}_{v}\big)(X)\Big]+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[\bar{f}_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}(\mathbb{E}_{v}\bar{f}_{v})(X)\Big]+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[f_{v}(X)-(\mathbb{E}_{v}f_{v})(X_{v})+h_{v}(X_{v})\,|\,X_{v}]

(24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}h_{v}(X)\Big]+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[f_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]

To estimate the lower bound,
we rely on own choice of
h_{v}
. By (
23
) we have

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: f_kSubstitute02}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}{\rm Var}\big[h_{v}(X)\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[f_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\Big({\rm Var}\big[h_{v}(X)\big]+\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[f_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\Big({\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{v}\bar{f}_{v})(X_{v})\big]+\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[\bar{f}_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}}\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}{\rm Var}[\bar{f}_{v}(X)].

As for the upper bound, we can apply Lemma
4.2

and repeat the same derivation as above to get

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: f_kSubstitute02}\leq

\displaystyle C_{\ref{lem:deg1T}}R\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}{\rm Var}\big[h_{v}(X)\big]+\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[f_{v}(X)\,|\,X_{v}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\ref{lem:deg1T}}R\sum_{v\in D_{m}(u)}{\rm Var}[\bar{f}_{v}(X)].

Therefore, by taking the constant
C
stated in the lemma to be the maximum of

C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}
and
C_{\ref{lem:deg1T}}
, the proof follows.

∎

4.2.
Proof of the base Case of Proposition
3.5

We now prove the base case of
Proposition
3.5
:

Lemma 4.9
.

There exists a constant
C=C(M,d)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
For any degree
1
function
f
with variables
(x_{u}\,:u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime}))
for some
\rho^{\prime}\in T
with
k\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
,

(25)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[\mathbb{E}\big[f(X)\,\big|\,X_{\rho^{\prime}}\big]\big]\leq CR^{4}({\rm h}^{\prime})^{2q}(d\lambda^{2})^{{\rm h}^{\prime}}{\rm Var}[f(X)].

where
{\rm h}^{\prime}={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})-k
.

Proof.

Let
f_{u}
for
u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
be the functions from Proposition
4.3
so that

(26)

\displaystyle f(X)=\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}f_{u}(X_{u})\mbox{ almost surely.}

We can assume
\mathbb{E}f(X)=0
and
\mathbb{E}f_{u}(X)=0
for every
u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
without affecting (
26
). From Proposition
4.3
, we have

\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}_{u}(X)]\leq C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}R^{3}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(X)],

where
C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}
denotes the constant
C
introduced in the Proposition.

We could apply Lemma
2.2
to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}f)^{2}(X_{\rho^{\prime}})\big]\leq

\displaystyle|D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})|\sum_{v\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}f_{v})^{2}(X_{\rho^{\prime}})\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})|C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}{\rm h}^{\prime 2q}\lambda^{2{\rm h}^{\prime}}\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}_{u}(X)]\leq C_{\ref{prop: CVbasis4}}C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}R^{4}({\rm h}^{\prime})^{2q}(d\lambda^{2})^{{\rm h}^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(X)]

where
C_{\ref{lem:Mbasis}}
denotes the constant
C
stated in the Lemma.
∎

Proof of the Base Case Proposition
3.5
.

Given
\rho^{\prime}\in T
and
0\leq m\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
described in the Proposition.
Let
{\rm h}^{\prime}={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})-m
.
By Lemma
4.9
, any function

f(x)=\sum_{v\in D_{m}(\rho^{\prime})}f_{v}(x_{v})
satisfies

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\prime}}f)(X)\big]\leq C_{\ref{lem:deg1}}R^{4}({\rm h}^{\prime})^{q}(d\lambda^{2})^{{\rm h}^{\prime}}{\rm Var}[f(X)],

where
C_{\ref{lem:deg1}}
denotes the
M
-dependent constant introduced in the Lemma.
With

\displaystyle C_{\ref{lem:deg1}}R^{4}({\rm h}^{\prime})^{q}(d\lambda^{2})^{{\rm h}^{\prime}}\leq C_{\ref{lem:deg1}}R^{4}({\rm h}^{\prime})^{q}\exp(-1.1\varepsilon{\rm h}^{\prime})\leq\exp\big(-\varepsilon({\rm h}^{\prime}-C_{1}(\log(R)+1))\big),

for some
C_{1}\geq 1
which depends on
M,d
.
∎

5.
Decomposition of Polynomials

In this section we study the representation of functions in terms of
\phi_{\sigma}
and
\psi_{\sigma}
.
Roughly speaking
\psi_{\sigma}
are “more orthogonal” than the
\phi_{\sigma}
. More formally we will show that under appropriate conditioning expections of
\psi_{\sigma}
factorize. Thus some of the effort in the proof and particularly in this section is devoted to relating the
\phi
and
\psi
representations and bounding moments of such representations.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assuming
d\lambda^{2}<1
and growth factor of
R
,
there exists
C=C(M,d)\geq 1
so that the following holds.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\bf 2}^{L}\backslash\emptyset
be a collection of subsets which is closed under decomposition.
Fix a positive integer
k_{1}
and
\rho^{\prime}\in T
with
l^{\prime}:={\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})>k_{1}
.
For every function
f
of the form

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{{\sigma:\sigma\neq 0}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{\leq\rho^{\prime}})}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)

with

\mathbb{E}f(X)=0,

here exists a decomposition of
f

f(X)=\sum_{u\leq\rho^{\prime}\,:\,{\rm h}(u)\geq k}\tilde{f}_{u}(X)\mbox{ almost surely,}

where,
for each
u\leq\rho^{\prime}
with
{\rm h}(u)\geq k_{1}
, we have a function
f_{u}(x)=f_{u}(x_{\leq u})
and

(1)

For
u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}
with
{\rm h}(u)>k_{1}
,
f_{u}(x)
is a linear combination of
\psi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{u})
and
\tilde{f}_{u}(x)=f_{u}(x)-\mathbb{E}f_{u}(X)
.

(2)

For
w\leq\rho^{\prime}
with
{\rm h}(w)\geq k_{1}
, we have

(27)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{CR^{3}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{u\in D_{k_{1}}(w)}f^{2}_{u}(X)\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}(\sum_{u\in D_{k_{1}}(w)}f_{u}(X))^{2}\leq CR\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{v\in D_{k_{1}}(w)}f^{2}_{u}(X)\Big].

We may group the
f_{u}
according to
{\rm h}(u)
and define for
k_{1}\leq k\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
,

f_{k}(x):=\sum_{u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})}\tilde{f}_{u}(x).

In other words,

f(X)=\sum_{k\in[k_{1},{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})]}f_{k}(X)\mbox{ almost surely.}

To prove the main lemma, let us begin by comparing
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
and
\psi_{\sigma}(x)
(See Definition
2.8
).

Lemma 5.2
.

For
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
,
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
can be expressed in the form

(28)

\displaystyle\phi_{\sigma}(x)=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I(\sigma)}\psi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x)-a_{\subset,{\sigma}}(x)-a_{<,{\sigma}}(x)-a_{c,{\sigma}},

where:

•

a_{\subset,{\sigma}}(x)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{{\sigma}^{\prime}}(x)
for
{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
such that
I(\sigma^{\prime})
is a proper subset of
I(\sigma)
.

•

a_{<,\sigma}(x)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x)
for
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{<\mathfrak{u}})
(recall that
\mathcal{B}_{<u}=\{S\in\mathcal{B}\,:\,\rho(S)<u\}
), and

•

a_{c,\sigma}
is a constant.

Proof.

Fix
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B})
and let
u=\rho(S)
and
S=S(\sigma)
. Recall that
I(\sigma):=\{i\in[d_{u}]\,:\,S(\sigma)\cap T_{u_{i}}\neq\emptyset\}
and

P_{{i}}\sigma\in[0,q-1]^{T}
is the projection of
\sigma
to
S_{i}
.
We can also decompose the function
\phi_{\sigma}
according to
\{{P_{{i}}\sigma}\}_{i\in I(\sigma)}
:

(29)

\displaystyle\phi_{\sigma}(x)=\prod_{i\in I(S)}\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x).

Before we proceed, let us note that by Lemma
1.11
and the definition of
\mathcal{B}
, we have
P_{{i}}\sigma\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\leq u_{i}})
.
Now, let us expand the function
\psi_{\sigma}
according to its definition:

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I(S)}\psi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x)=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I(S)}\Big(\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x)-\mathbb{E}\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(X)\Big)=\sum_{I_{1},I_{2}}\Big(\prod_{i\in I_{1}}\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x)\Big)\Big(\prod_{i\in I_{2}}(-\mathbb{E}\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(X))\Big),

where the summation is taken over all possible partition
I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}=I(\sigma)
.
Next, we can group the summands into four types based on
|I_{1}|
and
|I_{2}|
:

Type 1

|I_{1}|=|I(\sigma)|
.
The summand is simply
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
.

Type 2

2\leq|I_{1}|\leq|I(\sigma)|-1
.

Each summand is a constant multiple of
\phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(x)
where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the projection of
\sigma
to the indices
\sqcup_{i\in I_{1}}S_{i}
. Clearly,

S(\sigma^{\prime})=\sqcup_{i\in I_{1}}S_{i}
. With
|I_{1}|\geq 2
, we have
\rho(\sigma^{\prime})=u
.
Further, each
S_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq u_{i}}
for
i\in I(\sigma)
, it follows that

S(\sigma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}_{u}
, which in turn implies
\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{u})
.

We denote the sum of summands of this type by
a_{\subset,\sigma}(x)
.

Type 3

|I_{1}|=1
.
Each summand is a constant multiple of
\phi_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x)
,
where
i
is the element in
I_{1}
.
Notice that
P_{{i}}\sigma\in{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{A}_{<u})\subset{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{B}_{<u})
where the inclusion follows
from Lemma
1.11
.
We denote the sum of summands of this type as
a_{<,\sigma}(x)
.

Type 4

|I_{1}|=0

There is only one summand, which is a constant. We denote this constant by
a_{c,P_{{i}}\sigma}
.

With this decomposition, (
28
) follows.
∎

Given the expressions for
\psi_{\sigma}(x)
in terms
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
and vice-versa, for any given
u\in T\backslash L
, we can convert a linear combination
of
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})

to that of
\psi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For
u\in T\backslash L
, consider any function of the form

\displaystyle p_{u}(x)=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x).

Then there exists a decomposition

\displaystyle p_{u}(x)=

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{u}(x)+p_{<,u}(x)+c_{u},

where:

•

\tilde{f}_{u}(x)=f_{u}(x)-\mathbb{E}f_{u}(X)

and
f_{u}(x)
is a linear combination of
\psi_{\sigma}(x)
for
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
,

•

p_{<,u}(x)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{<u})
, and

•

c_{u}
is a constant.

Proof.

The decomposition is constructed through recursion on the following expression:

\displaystyle r(p_{u}):={\rm argmax}\big\{|I(\sigma)|\,:\,{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u}),\,c_{\sigma}\neq 0\big\}.

Suppose
r(p_{u})=2
. Then the statement of simply follows from Lemma
5.2
.

Suppose the statement of the lemma holds whenever
r(p_{u})\leq r
for
2\leq r<Rd
. Consider any function
p_{u}
with
r(p_{u})=r+1
:

p_{u}(x)=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})\,:|I(\sigma)|\leq r+1}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)=\underbrace{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})\,:|I(\sigma)|=r+1}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)}_{:=p_{u,r+1}(x)}+\underbrace{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})\,:|I(\sigma)|\leq r}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)}_{:=p_{u,\leq r}(x)}.

According to the decomposition of
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
in Lemma
5.2
, let

\displaystyle f_{u,r+1}(x):=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})\,:\,|I(\sigma)|=r+1}c_{\sigma}\psi_{\sigma}(x)

\displaystyle p_{*,u,r+1}(x):=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})\,:\,|I(\sigma)|=r+1}c_{\sigma}a_{*,{\sigma}}(x)

where
*
can be
\subset
,
<
, or
c
. Then,

(30)

\displaystyle p_{u,r+1}(x)=f_{u,r+1}(x)+p_{\subset,u,r+1}(x)+p_{<,u,r+1}(x)+p_{c,u,r+1}(x).

Observe that
p_{u,\leq r}(x)+p_{\subset,u,r+1}(x)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
and
|I(\sigma)|\leq r
.
Thus, by the inductive assumption, the summation can be expressed in the form

p_{u,\leq r}(x)+p_{\subset,u,r+1}(x)=\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{u}(x)+p^{\prime}_{<,u}(x)+c^{\prime}_{u}.

Finally, let

\displaystyle f_{u}(x)=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{u}(x)+f_{u,r+1}(x),

\displaystyle p_{<,u}(x)=

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{<,u}(x)+p_{<,u,r+1}(x),

\displaystyle c_{u}=

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{u}+p^{\prime}_{c,u}+\mathbb{E}\big[f_{u,r+1}(X)\big],

and we have

\displaystyle p_{u}(x)=

\displaystyle f_{u,r+1}(x)+p_{<,u,r+1}(x)+p_{c,u,r+1}(x)+\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{u}(x)+p^{\prime}_{<,u}(x)+c^{\prime}_{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{u}(x)+p_{<,u}(x)+c_{u}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

We will construct
f_{u}(x)
for
u
starting from top layer (
u=\rho^{\prime}
) to bottom layer.

For
k\in[k_{1},l^{\prime}-1]
, when
f_{u}(x)
is constructed for
u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}
with
{\rm h}(u)>k
, we define

(31)

\displaystyle f_{\leq k}(x)=f(x)-\sum_{u\,:\,{\rm h}(u)>k+1}\tilde{f}_{u}(x),

where
\tilde{f}_{u}(x)=f_{u}(x)-\mathbb{E}f_{u}(X)
.
Without lose of generality, let
f_{\leq l^{\prime}}(x)=f(x)
.

Fix
k\in[k_{1}+1,l^{\prime}]
. For the induction step, suppose
\{f_{u}(x)\}_{u\in T_{\rho^{\prime}}\,:\,{\rm h}(u)>k}

\{f_{\leq s}(x)\}_{s\in[k,l^{\prime}]}
have been constructed such that
f_{\leq k}(x)
can be expressed in the form

(32)

\displaystyle f_{\leq k}(x)=c^{\prime}+\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B})\,:\,k_{1}<{\rm h}(\rho(\sigma))\leq k}c^{\prime}_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)+\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}({\bf 2}^{L})\,:\,{\rm h}(\rho(\sigma))\leq k_{1}}c^{\prime}_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x).

Clearly, this holds when
k=l^{\prime}
.

For each
u
with
{\rm h}(u)=k
, let
p_{u}(x)=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})}c^{\prime}_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)
, and define
\tilde{f}_{u}(x),p_{<,u}(x)
, and
c_{u}
according to Lemma
5.3
.
Then,

\displaystyle f_{\leq k-1}(x)=

\displaystyle f_{\leq k}(x)-\sum_{u\,:\,{\rm h}(u)=k_{1}}\tilde{f}_{u}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c^{\prime}+\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B})\,:\,k_{1}<{\rm h}(\rho(\sigma))\leq k-1}c^{\prime}_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)+\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}({\bf 2}^{L})\,:\,{\rm h}(\rho(\sigma))\leq k_{1}}c^{\prime}_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{u\,:\,{\rm h}(u)=k}(p_{<,u}(x)+c_{u}).

Recall from Lemma
5.3
that
p_{<,u}(x)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{\sigma}(x)
with
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{<u})
, the function
f_{\leq k-1}(x)
satisfies (
32
) as well (with
k
been replaced by
k-1
).

Once the induction terminated at layer
k_{1}
, we obtain

f_{k_{1}}(x)=c+\sum_{u\in D_{k_{1}}(\rho^{\prime})}\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(2^{L_{u}}\backslash\emptyset)}c_{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}(x).

Now, observe that for
k_{1}<k\leq{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})
, we have
f_{k}(x)
is defined as the sum of
\tilde{f}_{u}
for
u\in D_{k}(\rho^{\prime})
, which are functions of mean
0
. Together with the assumption that
\mathbb{E}f(X)=0
, we have

\mathbb{E}f_{k_{1}}(X)=\mathbb{E}f(X)-\sum_{k=k_{1}+1}^{{\rm h}(\rho^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}f_{k}(X)=0.

Notice that
f_{k_{1}}
satisfies the assumption of the function stated in Lemma
4.7
with
m=k_{1}
.
By replacing
f_{k_{1}}
by
\bar{f}_{k_{1}}
and
f_{u}
by
\bar{f}_{u}
for each
u\in D_{k_{1}}(\rho^{\prime})
, the second statement follows while the third statement of the Lemma remains true. Hence, the proof is completed.
∎

6.
Induction Step 1: Decay of
f_{u}

The goal this section and next section is to prove Theorem
3.6
. Let us restate the theorem here.

Theorem
.

Given the rooted tree
T
and the transition matrix
M
described in Theorem
1.6
, and under
the additional assumption that
c_{M}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}M_{ij}>0
.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a collection of subsets satisfying Assumption
3.3
with parameters
{\rm h^{*}}
and
c^{*}
.
Then, there exists
C=C(M,d,c^{*})\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with parameters
{\rm h^{*}}+C(\log(R)+1)
and
c^{*}
.

In this and the following section
,
we will fix a collection
{\mathcal{A}}
that meets Assumption
3.3
with some parameters
l^{*}
and
c^{*}
. Additionally, we abbreviate

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}({\mathcal{A}}).

Further, we will fix
\rho^{\prime}\in T
and a function
f
described in the Assumption
3.3
, and assume

\mathbb{E}f(X)=0.

The proof is grounded in the decomposition of
f
as described in Lemma
5.1
,
which splits
f
into summation of
f_{k}
and subsequently into summations of
\tilde{f}_{u}
. Accordingly, this section is devoted to derive the variance decay properties of
f_{u}
stated as Proposition
6.1
below. The proposition will be used to derive variance decay properties of
f_{k}
, and toward the proof of Theorem
3.6
in next section.

Proposition 6.1
.

There exists
C=C(M,c^{*})\geq 1
so that the following holds.
For any
u\in T
, consider a function
f_{u}
of the form

f_{u}(x)=\sum_{0\neq{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{u})}c_{\sigma}\psi_{\sigma}(x).

Then, for
\theta\in[q]
, we have
the following bounds on
(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(x)

(recall that that by the
Markov Property
,
(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(x)
is a function of
x_{u}
):

(33)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})^{2}(\theta)\leq\exp(-2\varepsilon({\rm h}(u)-C(\log(R)+1)-{\rm h^{*}}))(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u}^{2})(\theta)

and

(34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u}\tilde{f}_{u})^{2}(X_{u})\big]\leq\exp(-2\varepsilon({\rm h}(u)-C(\log(R)+1)-{\rm h^{*}}))\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}^{2}_{u}(X).

Additionally, for any function
a(x)
having inputs involving only
(x_{v}\,:\,v\in T_{u_{i}})

for some
i\in[d_{u}]
:

(35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|\tilde{f}_{u}(X)a(X)|\big]\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}({\rm h}(u)-C(\log(R)+1)-{\rm h^{*}})\Big)(\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}^{2}_{u}(X))^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}a^{2}(X))^{1/2}.

Remark 6.2
.

The statement of the Proposition
6.1
is exactly the statement of Theorem
3.6
restricted to functions all of whose non-zero
c_{\sigma}
have
\rho(\sigma)=\rho^{\prime}
. Thus in some sense in this section we prove the Theorem for the most complex terms. And in the next section we will control the correlations between different terms.

This is an analogue in our setting to the classical fact in Fourier analysis that high amplitude functions have sharp decay under noise.

Remark 6.3
.

We remark that the proposition holds immediately whenever
|d_{u}|\leq 1
, since
\mathcal{B}_{u}=\emptyset
.

Before we proceed further, we need to decompose
f_{u}(x)
.

Definition 6.4
.

For
u\in T\backslash L
and
f_{u}(x)=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B}_{u})}c_{\sigma}\psi_{\sigma}(x)
, let

(36)

\displaystyle f_{u,I}(x):=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{u})\,:\,I(\sigma)=I}c_{\sigma}\psi_{\sigma}(x),\quad\mbox{and}

(37)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{u,I}(x):=

\displaystyle f_{u,I}(x)-\mathbb{E}f_{u,I}(X)

for each
I\subseteq[d_{u}]
with
|I|\geq 2
.

Given the above definition, we have

\displaystyle f_{u}(x)=\sum_{I\subseteq[d_{u}]\,:|I|\geq 2}f_{u,I}(x).

Proposition 6.5
.

There exists
C=C(M,c^{*})\geq 1
so that the following holds.
For any
u\in T\setminus L
and
I\subseteq[d_{u}]
with
|I|\geq 2
. Consider a function of the form

a(x)=\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{B}}_{u})\,:I(\sigma)=I}c_{\sigma}\psi_{\sigma}(x).

Then, for
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, let

U=T\setminus\big(\bigcup_{i\in I^{\prime}}T_{u_{i}}\big),

and we have

(38)

\displaystyle\big((\mathbb{E}_{U}a)(x)\big)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\exp\big(-\varepsilon|I^{\prime}|({\rm h}(u)-C{\rm h}^{*})\big)(\mathbb{E}_{U}a^{2})(x).

Roughly speaking the proposition states that under the decay of correlation in Assumption
3.3
, for functions all of whose coefficient
c_{\sigma}
have
S(\sigma)=I
for some large set
I
we get a variance decay of the form

\exp(-\epsilon|I|{\rm h}(u))
. For later applications the statement is more general allowing to condition on some of the subtrees. This is an analogue in our setting to the classical fact in Fourier analysis that high amplitude functions have sharp decay under noise.

Proof.

We fix
u\in T\backslash L
and
I\subseteq[d_{u}]
. Without lose of generality, we assume
I^{\prime}=[s]
.

Let
C_{0}=C_{0}(M,c^{*})
denote the constant described in the statement of the Proposition. The precise value of
C_{0}
will be determined during the proof.

For brevity, we introduce some notations that are only used in this proof.

(1)

Decomposition of
x\in[q]^{T}
:
Consider the representation of
x
as

x=(x_{u},x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{s}),

where,
\forall k\in[s]
,
x_{k}:=(x_{v}\,:\,v\leq u_{k})
, and
x_{0}=(x_{v}\,:v\in U\setminus\{u\})
.

Further, let

x_{\leq k}=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k}).

For
k\in[0,s]
,

a_{\leq k}(x_{\leq k}):=\mathbb{E}\Big[a(X)\,\Big|\,X_{u}=x_{u}\mbox{ and }X_{\leq k}=x_{\leq k}\Big].

Before we proceed to the proof, observe that applying Jenson’s inequality on conditional expectation,
we can form a chain of inequalities

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}_{U}a)^{2}(x)=(\mathbb{E}_{U}a_{0}^{2})(x)\leq(\mathbb{E}_{U}a_{1}^{2})(x)\leq(\mathbb{E}_{U}a_{2}^{2})(x)\leq\ldots\leq(\mathbb{E}_{U}a_{s}^{2})(x)=(\mathbb{E}_{U}a^{2})(x).

If
{\rm h}(u)\leq C_{0}+{\rm h^{*}}
, then the statement of the Proposition is weaker than
the inequality
(\mathbb{E}_{U}a)^{2}(x)\leq(\mathbb{E}_{U}a^{2})(x)
stated above. So the lemma follows immediately in that case. From now on we assume

(39)

\displaystyle{\rm h}(u)>C_{0}+{\rm h^{*}}.

We will improve each inequality in the above chain by leveraging the assumption (
11
).

Given the definition of
a(x)
,

\displaystyle a(x)=\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\prod_{i\in I\backslash[s]}\tilde{\phi}_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x_{0})\prod_{i\in[s]}\tilde{\phi}_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(x_{i})

By the
Markov Property
, the random variables
(X_{i}|X_{u}=x_{u})_{i\in[0,s]}
are independent. This gives rise to:

\displaystyle a_{\leq k}(x)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{{\sigma}}c_{\sigma}\prod_{i\in I\backslash[s]}\tilde{\phi}_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(X_{0})\prod_{i\in[s]}\tilde{\phi}_{P_{{i}}\sigma}(X_{i})\,\Big|\,X_{u}=x_{u}\mbox{ and }X_{\leq k}=x_{\leq k}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\sigma}}c_{\sigma}\underbrace{\prod_{i\in I\backslash[s]}\tilde{\phi}_{{P_{{i}}\sigma}}(x_{0})\prod_{i\in[k]}\tilde{\phi}_{{P_{{i}}\sigma}}(x_{k})}_{\mbox{ This part is freezed.}}\underbrace{\prod_{i\in[k+1,s]}(\mathbb{E}_{u}\tilde{\phi}_{{P_{{i}}\sigma}})(x_{u}).}_{\mbox{This part is a function of $x_{u}$}}

Now, fix
k\in[s]
and express
a_{\leq k}(x)=a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},x_{k})
. An essence of this proof is that the mapping:

\displaystyle y_{k}\mapsto a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},y_{k})

is a linear combination of
\tilde{\phi}_{\sigma}(y_{k})
with
\sigma\in{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{A}}_{u_{k}})
and the coefficients are functions of
(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1})
, which gives us room to apply the inductive assumption, or (
11
) from Assumption
3.3
.

To aid our analysis, we introduce
Y_{k}
, an independent copy of
X_{k}
.
By (
11
), we have

(40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})|Y_{u}]\big)^{2}\Big]\leq

\displaystyle\exp(-\varepsilon({\rm h}(u)-{\rm h^{*}}))\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\big].

The reason we introduce
Y_{k}
is that the L.H.S. and R.H.S. of the above inequality are not related
to (any moments of) conditional expectation of
a(X)
. However, it can still be used with some adjustment, relying on (
12
) from Assumption
3.3
.

Given the assumption on
C_{0}
being greater than or equal to
1
, we have

{\rm h}(u_{k})={\rm h}(u)-1\overset{\eqref{eq: Bwinwinhu}}{\geq}{\rm h^{*}}+C_{0}-1\geq{\rm h^{*}}.

Applying (
12
) to our function
y_{k}\mapsto a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},y_{k})

we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\big]\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{*}}\min_{\theta\in[q]}\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\big|Y_{u}=\theta\big]

(41)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\big|Y_{u}=x_{u}\big].

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\pi(x_{u})\big(\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})|Y_{u}=x_{u}]\big)^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})|Y_{u}]\big)^{2}\Big].

Combining the above expression, (
40
), and (
41
), we conclude that

(42)

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{E}\big[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\,\big|\,Y_{u}=x_{u}\big]\big)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{c^{*}\pi(x_{u})}\exp(-\varepsilon({\rm h}(u)-{\rm h^{*}}))\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\,\big|\,Y_{u}=x_{u}\big].

Notice that the expression inside the square in L.H.S. is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\,\big|\,Y_{u}=x_{u}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[a_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},X_{k})\,\big|\,X_{u}=x_{u}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[a_{\leq k}(X_{u},X_{\leq k-1},X_{k})\,\big|\,X_{u}=x_{u},X_{\leq k-1}=x_{\leq k-1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\leq k-1}(x).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Y_{k}}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},Y_{k})\,\big|\,Y_{u}=x_{u}\big]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[a^{2}_{\leq k}(x_{u},x_{\leq k-1},X_{k})\,\big|\,X_{u}=x_{u}\big]

\displaystyle=
