\pi_{\min}:=\min_{\theta\in[q]}\pi(\theta)
.

Further, in the case when
l=0
, then we simply have

\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\big]\geq\frac{1}{C_{0}}t^{2}
.

Proof.

We decompose
(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(x)
into three components:

(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(x)=\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(x_{u})+\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(x_{u})+\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})>l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(x_{u}),

where
\xi_{{\mathsf{w}}}^{(l)}
is introduced in Remark
A.6
.

For each
{\mathsf{w}}
with
r({\mathsf{w}})>l
,
\xi^{(l)}_{w}(X_{u})
is a function of
X_{v}

with
v={\mathfrak{p}}^{r({\mathsf{w}})-l}(u)
. Hence, the last component
\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})>l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(x_{u})
is a constant function whenever we condition on
X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}
. Consequently,

(111)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\big]=\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})+\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\Big].

From Proposition
A.5
, we know that

(112)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\Big]\geq\frac{t^{2}}{C_{0}},

where the constant
C_{0}
is the constant
C
stated in the Proposition.
Intuitively, from (
112
) it should be clear that if the R.H.S. of (
111
) is small, then

\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\Big]

cannot be small. Let us derive this with a coarse estimate.

By (
112
), we know there exists
\theta\in[q]
such that

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]\geq\frac{t^{2}}{C_{0}}.

Now, suppose

{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]<\frac{t^{2}}{4C_{0}}
.
We could apply triangle inequality to get

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})+\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]}-\sqrt{{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{t}{2C_{0}^{1/2}},

and together with (
111
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u}f_{u})(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\big]\geq

\displaystyle\pi(\theta){\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})+\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})=l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{\min}}{2C_{0}}t^{2}.

Consider the opposite case where

{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]\geq\frac{t^{2}}{4C_{0}}
.
First,

\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\Big[\sum_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}c_{w,u}\xi_{w}^{(l)}(X_{u})\,\Big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\theta\Big]\geq\frac{\pi_{\min}}{4C_{0}}t^{2}.

By applying the 4th property stated in Proposition
A.5
,
we conclude that

\max_{{\mathsf{w}}\,:r({\mathsf{w}})<l}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u}|\geq\frac{\sqrt{\pi_{\min}}}{2C_{0}}t.

In the case when
l=0
.
The argument is simpler, which follows directly from (
111
)
and the Proposition
A.5
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
A.25
.

Let
t_{0}=\max_{{\mathsf{w}},u\in L_{\rho^{\prime}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u}|

and let
{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{W}}
and
u^{\prime}\in L_{\rho^{\prime}}
be the pair such that

t_{0}=|c_{{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime},u^{\prime}}|
. Further, let
l_{0}=r({\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})
and
u_{0}={\mathfrak{p}}^{l}(u^{\prime})
.

If
l_{0}>0
, then we have

|c_{{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime},u_{0}}|=\sum_{v\in L_{u}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime},v}|\geq|c_{{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime},u^{\prime}}|=t_{0},

where the first equality follows from the assumptions of the coefficients.
We will try to construct a sequence of triples
(l_{k},t_{k},u_{k})
indexed by
k
such that
(l_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is strictly decreasing such that

{\mathsf{W}}_{l_{k}}\neq\emptyset
,
{\rm h}(u_{k})=l_{k}
, and
t_{k}=\max_{{\mathsf{w}}\in{\mathsf{W}}_{l_{k}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}|
.

Suppose we have a triple
(l_{k},t_{k},u_{k})
such that
l_{k}\geq 0
,
{\rm h}(u_{k})=l_{k}
,
{\mathsf{W}}_{l_{k}}\neq\emptyset
, and
t_{k}=\max_{{\mathsf{w}}\in{\mathsf{W}}_{l_{k}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}|
for some index
k\geq 0
.

We apply Lemma
A.26
to get

(1)

Either
\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u_{k}}f_{u_{k}})(X_{u_{k}})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u_{k})}\big]\geq\frac{\pi_{\min}}{2C_{0}}t_{k}^{2}
, or

(2)

\max_{{\mathsf{w}}\in{\mathsf{W}}_{<\ell_{k}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}|\geq\frac{\sqrt{\pi_{\min}}}{2C_{0}}t_{k}
. (This case cannot happen if
\ell_{k}=0
.)

If the first case is true, then we terminate the process of finding next triple
(\ell_{k+1},t_{k+1},u_{k+1})
.

If the second case is true, let
{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathsf{W}}
be the vertex such that
|c_{{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime\prime},u_{k}}|=\max_{{\mathsf{w}}\in{\mathsf{W}}_{<\ell_{k}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}|
and set
\ell_{k+1}=r(w^{\prime\prime})
. Since

c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}=\sum_{u\in D_{\ell_{k+1}}(u_{k})}c_{{\mathsf{w}},u},

we have

(113)

\displaystyle t_{k+1}:=\max_{u\in T_{u_{k}}\,:\,{\rm h}(u)=\ell_{k+1}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u}|\geq\frac{1}{Rd^{\ell_{k}-\ell_{k+1}}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u_{k}}|=\frac{1}{Rd^{\ell_{k}-\ell_{k+1}}}t_{k}.

Further, let
u_{k+1}={\rm argmax}_{u\in T_{u_{k}}\,:\,{\rm h}(u)=\ell_{k+1}}|c_{{\mathsf{w}},u}|
.

In this way, we produce a new triple satisfying the same assumption as
(l_{k},t_{k},u_{k})
described above.

Since
l_{0}>l_{1}>l_{2}\dots
is a monotone decreasing chain of non-negative number, it means this argument must terminated in
r_{0}
steps. Now, suppose it terminates at the
k
-th step, resulting a triple
(l_{k},t_{k},u_{k})
, and

\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{u_{k}}f_{u_{k}})(X_{u_{k}})\,\big|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u_{k})}\big]\geq\frac{\pi_{\rm min}}{2C_{0}}t_{k}^{2}\overset{\eqref{eq: CVbasis200}}{\geq}\frac{\pi_{\min}}{2C_{0}}\big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi_{\min}}}{2C_{0}}Rd\big)^{-2r_{0}}t_{0}^{2}.

On the other hand, from Proposition
A.5
,

\sum_{u\in L_{\rho^{\prime}}}{\rm Var}[f_{u}(X_{u})]\leq CRd^{r_{0}}t_{0}^{2}.

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in L_{\rho^{\prime}}}{\rm Var}[f_{u}(X_{u})]\leq C(M,d)R^{2r_{0}+1}\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}\big[f(X)\,|\,X_{\rho^{\prime}}\big].

∎

Appendix B
Properties of Markov Chains and Galton-Watson Tree

B.1.
Markov Chains

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Let
\theta_{1}=\text{argmin}_{\theta\in[q]}h(\theta)

and
\theta_{2}=\text{argmax}_{\theta\in[q]}h(\theta)
. (In the case of a tie, we may choose any of the minimizers or maximizers.)
First, we have

{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})]\leq(h(\theta_{2})-h(\theta_{1}))^{2}.

Next, for any
\beta\in[q]
, we have

\max\left\{|\mathbb{E}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta]-h(\theta_{1})|,\,|\mathbb{E}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta]-h(\theta_{2})|\right\}\geq\frac{1}{2}|h(\theta_{2})-h(\theta_{1})|.

Let
i\in\{1,2\}
be the index such that
|\mathbb{E}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta]-h(\theta_{i})|\geq\frac{1}{2}|h(\theta_{2})-h(\theta_{1})|
,
and we will use this together with
c_{M}>0
to give a lower bound on the conditional variance:

\displaystyle{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta]\geq

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{E}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta]-h(\theta_{i})\big)^{2}\mathbb{P}\{X_{u}=\theta_{i}\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}=\beta\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(h(\theta_{2})-h(\theta_{1}))^{2}c_{M}.

Since it holds for every
\beta\in[q]
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})\,|\,X_{{\mathfrak{p}}(u)}]\geq\frac{1}{4}(h(\theta_{2})-h(\theta_{1}))^{2}c_{M}\geq\frac{c_{M}}{4}{\rm Var}[h(X_{u})].

∎

B.1.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.2

Recall that real Jordon Canonical form of
M
is a
q\times q
diagonal block matrix

{\bf J}={\rm diag}({\bf J}_{0},{\bf J}_{1},\dots,{\bf J}_{s_{1}})

for some
s_{1}\leq q
.

Since
M
is ergodic, the eigenspace corresponds to eigenvalue
1
is 1-dimensional. Thus,
We may assume
{\bf J}_{0}=[1]
is the unique Jordan block corresponds to eigenvalue
1
.

For each
s\in[1,s_{1}]
,
{\bf J}_{s}
is either a
m_{s}\times m_{s}
matrix of the form

{\bf J}_{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{s}&1&&\\
&\lambda_{s}&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&1\\
&&&\lambda_{s}\end{bmatrix}

for some
\lambda_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
|\lambda_{s}|\leq\lambda
;
or a
J_{s}
is a
2m_{s}\times 2m_{s}
matrix of the form

{\bf J}_{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{s}R_{s}&I_{2}&&\\
&\lambda_{s}R_{s}&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&I_{2}\\
&&&\lambda_{s}R_{s}\end{bmatrix}
,
where
|\lambda_{s}|\leq\lambda
, and

R_{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\theta_{s})&\sin(\theta_{s})\\
-\cos(\theta_{s})&\sin(\theta_{s})\end{bmatrix}
is a rotation matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with parameter
\theta_{s}\in(0,2\pi)
.
In the later case, it corresponds to the conjugate pair of eigenvalues

\lambda_{s}(\cos(\theta_{s})\pm{\bf i}\sin(\theta_{s}))

According to Jordon Decomposition, there exists an invertible matrix
P
such
that
M=P{\bf J}P^{-1}
.

For
i\in[1,q-1]
, let
\phi_{i}
be the
i+1
th column of
P
. Because
P
is invertible,
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
form a linear basis of functions from
[q]
to
\mathbb{R}
.

Since
\pi
is a left-eigenvector of
M
with eigenvalue
1
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\pi}\phi_{i}(Y)=\pi^{\top}\phi_{i}=0,

because
\phi_{i}
is a sum of up to two generalized eigenvectors with eigenvalues not equal to
1
.

(A generalized eigenvector
v
with eigenvalue
\lambda^{\prime}
of
M
is a vector
which satisfies
(M-\lambda^{\prime})^{k}v=\vec{0}
for some positive integer
k
.
Whenever
\lambda^{\prime}\neq 1
,

\pi^{\top}v=(\frac{1}{(1-\lambda^{\prime})^{k}}\pi^{\top}(M-\lambda^{\prime})^{k})v=\frac{1}{(1-\lambda^{\prime})^{k}}\pi^{\top}\cdot\vec{0}=0.

If index
i
corresponds to
{\bf J}_{s}
which associated with a real eigenvalue,
then
\phi_{i}
is a generalized eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda_{s}
;
And if
{\bf J}_{s}
associates with a complex conjugate pair or eigenvalues,
then
\phi_{i}
is a sum of two generalized eigenvectors with eigenvalues

\lambda_{s}(\cos(\theta_{s})+{\bf i}\sin(\theta_{s}))

and
\lambda_{s}(\cos(\theta_{s})-{\bf i}\sin(\theta_{s}))
, respectively. )
As a consequence, every function
f:[q]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
can be uniquely decomposed in the form

(114)

\displaystyle f=\mathbb{E}f+\sum_{i\in[q-1]}\delta_{i}\phi_{i}.

With this unique decomposition, let us define a semi-norm

\|f\|_{M}=\max_{i\in[q-1]}|\delta_{i}|.

Lemma B.1
.

There exists
C>0
so that for every
f:[q]\to\mathbb{R}
,

(115)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\|f\|_{M}^{2}\leq{\rm Var}_{Y\sim\pi}(f(Y))\leq C\|f\|^{2}_{M}.

Proof.

Without lose of generality, let
f=\sum_{i\in[2,q]}\delta_{i}\phi_{i}
,
since both
\|f\|_{M}
and
{\rm Var}_{Y\sim\pi}(f(Y))

are invariant under a constant shift.

Let
D_{\pi}={\rm diag}(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{q})
. Also, let
\vec{\delta}=(0,\delta_{2},\dots,\delta_{q})
. Then,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{M}=

\displaystyle\|\vec{\delta}\|_{\infty}

and

\displaystyle{\rm Var}_{Y\sim\pi}(f(Y))=

\displaystyle\vec{\delta}^{\top}P^{\top}D_{\pi}P\vec{\delta}.

Let
s_{\rm max}
and
s_{\rm min}

be the maximum and minimum singular value of
P^{\top}D_{\pi}P
, respectively.
Together with

q^{-1/2}\|\vec{\delta}\|_{2}\leq\|\vec{\delta}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\vec{\delta}\|_{2}
,
we have

(116)

\displaystyle s^{2}_{\rm min}q^{-1}\|f\|^{2}_{M}\leq s^{2}_{\rm min}q^{-1}\|\vec{\delta}\|^{2}_{2}\leq{\rm Var}_{Y\sim\pi}(f(Y))\leq s^{2}_{\rm max}\|\vec{\delta}\|^{2}_{2}\leq s^{2}_{\rm max}\|f\|^{2}_{M}.

If
s_{\rm min}>0
, then we can complete the proof by taking
C=\max\{s_{\rm max}^{2},q/s^{2}_{\rm min}\}
. It remains to show that
s_{\rm min}>0
, or equvialently
P^{\top}D_{\pi}P
is invertible.
Because
M
is ergodic, each entry of
\pi
is positive, and thus
D_{\pi}
is invertible.
Hence,
P^{\top}D_{\pi}P
is invertible because it is a product of three invertible matrices.

∎

Lemma B.2
.

There exists
C>0
so that for every
f:[q]\to\mathbb{R}
,

(117)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\|f\|_{M}\leq\|f-\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\pi}f(Y)\|_{\infty}\leq C\|f\|_{M}.

Proof.

This simply follows from both
\|f\|_{M}
and
\|f-\mathbb{E}f\|_{\infty}
are both norms
on the finite dimensional space
\{f:[q]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\pi}f(Y)=0\}
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

There exists
C\geq 1
depending on
M
such that
For any function
f:[q]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(118)

\displaystyle\|M^{k}f\|_{M}\leq Ck^{q}\lambda^{k}\|f\|_{M}.

Remark B.4
.

Notice that
M^{k}f
can be interpreted as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[f(X_{u})\,\big|\,X_{\mathfrak{p}^{k}(u)}=i\big]=(M^{k}f)(i),

for every
u\in T
where
\mathfrak{p}^{k}(u)
is well-defined.

Proof.

(119)

\displaystyle\|M^{k}f\|_{M}=

\displaystyle\|P{\bf J}^{k}(\sum_{i\in[q]}\delta_{i}e_{i})\|_{M}=\|{\bf J}^{k}(\sum_{i\in[2,q]}\delta_{i}e_{i})\|_{\infty}.\leq q\max_{i\in[2,q]}\|\delta_{i}\|\max_{i,j\in[2,q]}|{\bf J}^{k}_{ij}|.

Notice that
{\bf J}^{k}
is the diagonal block matrix whose blocks
{\bf J}_{s}^{k}
for
s\in[s_{1}]
. The block
{\bf J}^{k}_{s}
can be computed directly:
In the case when
{\bf J}_{s}
corresponds to a complex conjugate pair of eigenvalues,

(120)

\displaystyle{\bf J}^{k}_{s}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda^{k}_{s}R_{s}^{k}&{k\choose 1}\lambda^{k-1}_{s}R^{k-1}_{s}&\dots&{k\choose m_{s}-1}\lambda^{k-m_{s}+1}_{s}R^{k-m_{s}+1}_{s}\\
&\lambda^{k}_{s}R^{k}_{s}&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&\ddots&{k\choose 1}\lambda^{k-1}_{s}R^{k-1}_{s}\\
&&&\lambda^{k}_{s}R^{k}_{s},\end{bmatrix}

where we treat
{k\choose r}=0
if
r>k
. It can be verified directly by induction, relying on the identity
{k\choose r-1}+{k\choose r}={k+1\choose r}
.
Further, removing the
R_{s}
terms in the above equation we obtain the formula for

{\bf J}^{k}_{s}
when
{\bf J}_{s}
corresponds to a real eigenvalue.

Therefore, with
{k\choose q}\leq k^{q}
,
|\lambda_{s}^{r}|\leq\lambda^{r}
, and

\max_{i,j}{R_{s}^{r}}_{ij}\ <1
for
r\geq 1
, we obtain the bound

(121)

\displaystyle\max_{s\in[2,q]}\max_{i,j\in[q]}|({\bf J_{s}}^{k})_{ij}|\leq C^{\prime}k^{q}\lambda^{k},

where
C^{\prime}
is a constant which depends on
q
and
\lambda
.

Now we substitute the above bound into (
119
) to get

\displaystyle\|M^{k}f\|_{M}\leq qC^{\prime}k^{q}\lambda^{k}\|f\|_{M}.

The proof is completed by taking
C=qC^{\prime}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

The proof of Lemma
2.2
follows from the
\|\cdot\|_{M}
decay from
Lemma
B.3
and that both
{\rm Var}[f]
and
\|f-\mathbb{E}f\|_{\infty}
are comparable to
\|f\|_{M}
within a constant multiplicative factor
(Lemma
B.1
and Lemma
B.2
).
∎
